**Oceania**[^cite_note-5] is a [geographical region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Region)[^cite_note-6] including [Australasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasia), [Melanesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesia), [Micronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micronesia), and [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia).[^cite_note-aging-7][^cite_note-worldatlas-8]

Spanning the [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Hemisphere) and [Western](73162.md) hemispheres, at the centre of the [water hemisphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_and_water_hemispheres), Oceania is estimated to have a land area of about 9,000,000 square kilometres (3,500,000 sq mi) and a population of around 46.3 million as of 2024, when including [Australia](4689264.md) but excluding the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago). When compared against other continental land areas, Oceania is the smallest and the [second-least populated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_continents_and_continental_subregions_by_population) after [Antarctica](18959138.md). It is home to Earth's third-largest remaining area of tropical rainforest, which covers much of the island of [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea).[^cite_note-9]

Oceania has a diverse mix of economies from the [highly developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) and globally competitive [financial markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_market) of [Australia](4689264.md), [French Polynesia](10737.md), [Hawaii](13270.md), [New Caledonia](21342.md), and [New Zealand](4913064.md), which rank high in [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life) and [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index),[^cite_note-worlda-10][^cite_note-herita-11] to the much [less developed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Least_developed_countries) economies of [Kiribati](16674.md), [Papua New Guinea](22943.md), [Tuvalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuvalu), [Vanuatu](32443.md), and [Western New Guinea](1432822.md).[^cite_note-imforg-12] The largest and most populous [country in Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_and_dependent_territories_in_Oceania) is Australia, and the largest city is [Sydney](27862.md).[^cite_note-dfatgov-13] [Puncak Jaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puncak_Jaya) in [Indonesia](14579.md) is the highest peak in Oceania at 4,884 m (16,024 ft).[^cite_note-modern283-14]

The first settlers of Australia, New Guinea, and the large islands just to the east arrived more than 60,000 years ago.[^cite_note-15] Oceania was [first explored by Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exploration_of_the_Pacific) from the 16th century onward. Portuguese explorers, between 1512 and 1526, reached the [Tanimbar Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanimbar_Islands), some of the [Caroline Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Islands), and west New Guinea. Spanish and Dutch explorers followed, then British and French. On his first voyage in the 18th century, [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook), who later arrived at the highly developed [Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Islands), went to [Tahiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahiti) and followed the [east coast of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_states_of_Australia) for the first time.[^cite_note-foundi-16] The [arrival of European settlers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europeans_in_Oceania) in subsequent centuries resulted in a significant alteration in the social and political landscape of Oceania. The [Pacific theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_history_of_Oceania#World_War_II) saw major action during the [First](4764461.md) and [Second World Wars](32927.md).

The [rock art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australian_art#Stone_art) of [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md) is the longest continuously practiced artistic tradition in the world.[^cite_note-oceanart-17] Most Oceanian countries are [parliamentary democracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_democracy), with tourism serving as a large source of income for the [Pacific island nations](341111.md).[^cite_note-newpol162-18]

## Etymology

Oceania is from the [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) *océanie* coming from [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) Okeanòs, Ωκεανός, ocean. It was coined in 1812 by geographer [Conrad Malte-Brun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conrad_Malte-Brun).[^cite_note-19][^cite_note-20]

## Definition

### Characteristics



Oceania with its sovereign states and dependent territories within the subregions [Australasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasia), [Melanesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesia), [Micronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micronesia), and [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia)

Definitions of Oceania vary.[^cite_note-21][^cite_note-asianpacific-22][^cite_note-worldatlas-8] The broadest definition encompasses the islands between mainland [Asia](689.md) and the [Americas](29833.md).[^cite_note-aging-7][^cite_note-23][^cite_note-harv-24] The island nation of [Australia](4689264.md) is the only piece of land in the area which is large enough to typically be considered a continent, as [Zealandia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zealandia) is usually considered a micro-continent.[^cite_note-25][^cite_note-26] The culture of the people who lived on these islands was often distinct from that of Asia and [pre-Columbian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Columbian_era) America.[^cite_note-edu_au-27] Before [Europeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnic_groups_in_Europe) arrived in the area, the sea shielded Australia and south central [Pacific islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_islands) from cultural influences that spread through large continental landmasses and adjacent islands.[^cite_note-edu_au-27][^cite_note-cam_uk-28] The islands of the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago), north of Australia, mainly lie on the [continental shelf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continental_shelf) of Asia, and their inhabitants had more exposure to mainland Asian culture as a result of this closer proximity.[^cite_note-edu_au-27]



[Mercator](20771.md) Planisphere by A.-H. Brué (1816), showing *Océanie*, the *Grand Océan*, and *Polynésie* including all the islands of the Pacific Ocean

The geographer [Conrad Malte-Brun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conrad_Malte-Brun) coined the [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) expression *Terres océaniques* ('Oceanic lands') c. 1804.[^cite_note-OED-29] In 1814 another French cartographer, Adrien-Hubert Brué, coined from this expression the shorter *Océanie*,[^cite_note-30] which derives from the [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin) word *[oceanus](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/oceanus#Latin)*, and this from the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) word [ὠκεανός](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/%E1%BD%A0%CE%BA%CE%B5%CE%B1%CE%BD%CF%8C%CF%82#Ancient_Greek) (*ōkeanós*), 'ocean'. The term *Oceania* is used because, unlike the other continental groupings, it is the ocean that links the parts of the region together.[^cite_note-31] John Eperjesi's 2005 book *The Imperialist Imaginary* says that Since the mid-19th century, [Western](21208200.md) cartographers have used the term *Oceania* to organize and classify the Pacific region.[^cite_note-32]



1852 map by Jean-Denis Barbié du Bocage. Includes regions of [Polynesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia), [Micronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micronesia), [Melanesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melanesia), and [Malesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malesia)

In the 19th century, many geographers divided Oceania into mostly racially based subdivisions: *Australasia*, *[Malesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malesia)* (encompassing the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago)), *Melanesia*, *Micronesia*, and *Polynesia*. The 2011 book *Maritime Adaptations of the Pacific*, by Richard W. Casteel and Jean-Claude Passeron, states that Oceania has traditionally been considered a continent in anthropological studies, similar to Africa, Asia, and the Americas.[^cite_note-33] [Bartholomew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Bartholomew) described Oceania as one of six major world divisions, including Australia and Pacific islands.[^cite_note-34] American author [Samuel Griswold Goodrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_Griswold_Goodrich) wrote in his 1854 book *History of All Nations* that, some 19th-century geographers classified the Pacific islands as a third continent called Oceania, alongside the New and Old Worlds. In this book, the other two continents were categorized as being the New World (the Americas) and the [Old World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_World) ([Afro-Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia)).[^cite_note-35] In his 1879 book *Australasia*, British naturalist [Alfred Russel Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Russel_Wallace) commented that, geographers commonly used *Oceania* to refer to the Pacific islands, with Australia as its central landmass.[^cite_note-austral-36] He did not explicitly label Oceania a continent in the book, but did note that it was one of the six major divisions of the world.[^cite_note-austral-36] *The Oxford Handbook of World History* (2011) describes the Oceania is often treated as a secondary topic in world history, appearing at the end of global narratives as a marginal region.[^cite_note-37]

In most non-[English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language)-speaking countries, Oceania is treated as a continent in the sense that it is "one of the parts of the world", and Australia is only seen as an island nation. In other non-English-speaking countries Australia and [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia) are thought of as continents, while Asia, Europe, and Oceania are regarded as "parts of the world".

[^cite_note-38]

[^cite_note-39]

&#x20;Various writers from [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language)-speaking countries have also described Oceania as a continent over the years.

[^cite_note-40]

[^cite_note-41]

[^cite_note-42]

[^cite_note-43]

[^cite_note-44]

[^cite_note-45]

[^cite_note-46]

&#x20;Prior to the 1950s, before the popularization of the theory of [plate tectonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_tectonics), [Antarctica](18959138.md), Australia, and [Greenland](12118.md) were sometimes described as island continents, but none were usually taught as one of the world's continents in the English-speaking countries.

[^cite_note-47]

[^cite_note-48]

[^cite_note-lewis32b-49]

. In his 1961 book *The United States and the Southwest Pacific*, American author [Clinton Hartley Grattan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C._Hartley_Grattan) commented that, by 1961, the term *Oceania* to describe Australia, [New Zealand](4913064.md), and the Pacific Islands was considered somewhat outdated.

[^cite_note-50]

&#x20;Australia is a founding member of the [Pacific Islands Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islands_Forum) in 1971, and at times has been interpreted as the largest Pacific island.

[^cite_note-'O'Malley_SMH_21_Sep_2014-51]

[^cite_note-52]

&#x20;Some geographers group the [Australian tectonic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Plate) with others in the Pacific to form a geological continent.

[^cite_note-53]

&#x20;*[National Geographic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Geographic)* defines Oceania as a continent based on its connection to the Pacific Ocean rather than landmass.

[^cite_note-54]

&#x20;Others have labelled it as the "liquid continent".

[^cite_note-55]

[^cite_note-56]

[^cite_note-57]

&#x20;The Pacific Ocean itself has been labelled as a "continent of islands", and contains approximately 25,000, which is more than all the other major oceans combined.

[^cite_note-58]

[^cite_note-59]

&#x20;In a 1991 article, American archeologist Toni L. Carrell wrote, The vast size and distances within the [Pacific Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Rim) make it challenging to view it as a single geographical unit.

[^cite_note-nps-60]

&#x20;Oceania's subregions of Australasia, Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia cover two major plates; the Australian Plate (also known as the [Indo-Australian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-Australian_Plate)) and the [Pacific Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Plate), in addition to two minor plates; the [Nazca Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazca_Plate) and the [Philippine Sea Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_Sea_Plate).

[^cite_note-birds-61]

[^cite_note-plates-62]

&#x20;The Australian Plate includes Australia, [Fiji](10707.md), [New Caledonia](21342.md), [Papua New Guinea](22943.md), [Vanuatu](32443.md), and parts of New Zealand.

[^cite_note-birds-61]

[^cite_note-plates-62]

&#x20;The Pacific Plate covers the [Solomon Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_Islands_\(archipelago\)) and parts of New Zealand, as well as Micronesia (excluding the westernmost islands near the Philippine Sea Plate) and Polynesia (excluding Easter Island).

[^cite_note-birds-61]

[^cite_note-plates-62]

&#x20;The Nazca Plate, which includes Easter Island, neighbours the [South American Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_American_Plate), and is still considered to be a separate tectonic plate, despite only containing a handful of islands.

[^cite_note-birds-61]

[^cite_note-plates-62]



Map displaying parts of [Near Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_Oceania) and [Remote Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remote_Oceania) with a focus on [Efate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efate)

The new terms [Near Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Near_Oceania) and [Remote Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remote_Oceania) were proposed in 1973 by anthropologists [Roger Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Curtis_Green) and [Andrew Pawley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andrew_Pawley). By their definition, Near Oceania consists of New Guinea, the Bismarck Archipelago, and the Solomon Islands, except the [Santa Cruz Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santa_Cruz_Islands).[^cite_note-63] They are designed to dispel the outdated categories of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia; many scholars now replace those categories with Green's terms since the early 1990s, but the old categories are still used in science, popular culture and general usage.[^cite_note-64]

### Boundaries

Further information on Oceania borders: [Boundaries between the continents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boundaries_between_the_continents) and [List of transcontinental countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_transcontinental_countries)

Islands that are generally considered to be at the geographic extremes of Oceania are: the [Bonin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonin_Islands), a politically integral part of [Japan](15573.md); [Hawaii](13270.md), a state of the [United States](3434750.md); [Clipperton Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clipperton_Island), a possession of [France](5843419.md); [Rapa Nui (Easter Island)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island), belonging to [Chile](5489.md); and [Macquarie Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macquarie_Island), belonging to Australia.[^cite_note-class-65][^cite_note-66][^cite_note-ausgov-67][^cite_note-68][^cite_note-realm-69][^cite_note-70][^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71]

#### United Nations interpretation

Main article: [United Nations geoscheme for Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_geoscheme_for_Oceania)

The [United Nations](31769.md) (UN) has used its own geopolitical definition of Oceania since its foundation in 1947, which utilizes four of the five subregions from the 19th century: Australasia, Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia. This definition consists of discrete political entities and so excludes the Bonin Islands, Hawaii, Clipperton Island, and the Juan Fernández Islands, along with Easter Island, which was annexed by Chile in 1888.[^cite_note-stats-72] It is used in statistical reports, by the [International Olympic Committee](15147.md), and by many atlases.[^cite_note-Lewis_1997_32-73] The UN categorizes Oceania, and by extension the Pacific area, as one of the major continental divisions of the world, along with Africa, Asia, Europe, and the Americas. Their definition includes [American Samoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Samoa), Australia and their [external territories](4689096.md), the [Cook Islands](7067.md), [Federated States of Micronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_States_of_Micronesia), [French Polynesia](10737.md), Fiji, [Guam](11974.md), [Kiribati](16674.md), the Marshall Islands, [Nauru](21302.md), New Caledonia, New Zealand, [Niue](21228.md), the [Northern Mariana Islands](21402.md), [Palau](23372.md), Papua New Guinea, [Pitcairn Islands](23450.md), [Samoa](27238.md), the Solomon Islands, [Tokelau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokelau), [Tonga](30158.md), [Tuvalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuvalu), [Vanuatu](32443.md), [Wallis and Futuna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallis_and_Futuna), and the [United States Minor Outlying Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Minor_Outlying_Islands) ([Baker Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baker_Island), [Howland Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howland_Island), [Jarvis Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarvis_Island), [Midway Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midway_Atoll), [Palmyra Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_Atoll), and [Wake Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wake_Island)).[^cite_note-stats-72] The original UN definition of Oceania from 1947 included these same countries and semi-independent territories, which were mostly still [colonies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony) at that point.[^cite_note-1950census-74] Hawaii had not yet become a U.S. state in 1947, and as such was part of the original UN definition of Oceania. The island states of Indonesia, Japan, the Philippines, [Singapore](27318.md), and Taiwan, all located within the bounds of the Pacific or associated marginal seas, are excluded from the UN definition; already in 1947 [Dutch New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_New_Guinea) was counted as part of Asia.[^cite_note-1950census-74] The states of [Hong Kong](13404.md) and [Malaysia](3607937.md), located in both mainland Asia and marginal seas of the Pacific, are also excluded, as are [Brunei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei), [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor), and [Indonesian New Guinea/Western New Guinea](1432822.md).[^cite_note-75][^cite_note-76] [The CIA World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Factbook) also categorizes Oceania as one of the major continental divisions of the world, but the name "Australia and Oceania" is used. Their definition does not include all of Australia's external territories, but is otherwise the same as the UN's definition, and is also used for statistical purposes.[^cite_note-77] The Pacific Islands Forum expanded during the early 2010s, and areas that were already included in the UN definition of Oceania, such as French Polynesia, gained membership.[^cite_note-78]

#### Early interpretations



A German map of Oceania from 1884, showing the region to encompass Australia and all islands between Asia and [Latin America](18524.md)

French writer [Gustave d'Eichthal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gustave_d'Eichthal) remarked in 1844 that Oceania's boundaries extended across the entire Pacific Ocean.[^cite_note-eic-79] [Conrad Malte-Brun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conrad_Malte-Brun) in 1824 defined Oceania as covering Australia, New Zealand, the islands of Polynesia (which then included all the Pacific islands) and the Malay Archipelago.[^cite_note-80][^cite_note-81] [Worcester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Emerson_Worcester) described Oceania as a collection of numerous Pacific islands, categorizing it as one of the world's five major divisions. He also viewed Oceania as covering Australia, New Zealand, the Malay Archipelago and the islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia.[^cite_note-82] In 1887, the [Royal Asiatic Society of Great Britain and Ireland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Asiatic_Society_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland) referred to Australia as the area's westernmost land,[^cite_note-83] while in 1870, British Reverend [Alexander Mackay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Murdoch_Mackay) identified the [Bonin Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonin_Islands) as its northernmost point, and [Macquarie Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macquarie_Island) as its southernmost point.[^cite_note-mac-84] The Bonin Islands at that time were a possession of Britain; Macquarie Island, to the south of [Tasmania](29944.md), is a subantarctic island in the Pacific. It was politically associated with Australia and Tasmania by 1870.[^cite_note-85]

[Alfred Russel Wallace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Russel_Wallace) believed in 1879 that Oceania extended to the [Aleutian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleutian_Islands), which are among the northernmost islands of the Pacific.[^cite_note-austral-36] The islands, now politically associated with [Alaska](624.md), have historically had [inhabitants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleut) that were related to [Indigenous Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_the_Americas), in addition to having non-tropical biogeography similar to that of Alaska and [Siberia](29639.md).[^cite_note-86][^cite_note-87] Wallace insisted while the surface area of this wide definition was greater than that of Asia and Europe combined, the land area was only a little greater than that of Europe.[^cite_note-austral-36] American geographer Sophia S. Cornell claimed that the Aleutian Islands were not part of Oceania in 1857.[^cite_note-corn-88] She stated that Oceania was divided up into three groups; Australasia (which included Australia, New Zealand, and the Melanesian islands), [Malesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malesia) (which included all present-day countries within the Malay Archipelago, not the modern country of Malaysia) and Polynesia (which included both the Polynesian and Micronesian islands in her definition).[^cite_note-corn-88] Aside from mainland Australia, areas that she identified as of high importance were Borneo, Hawaii, Indonesia's [Java](69336.md) and [Sumatra](28679.md), New Guinea, New Zealand, the Philippines, French Polynesia's [Society Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Islands), Tasmania, and Tonga.[^cite_note-corn-88]

American geographer [Jesse Olney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jesse_Olney)'s 1845 book *A Practical System of Modern Geography* defined Oceania as consisting of the many islands of the Pacific located southeast of Asia. Olney divided up Oceania into three groups; Australasia (which included Australia, New Guinea, and New Zealand), Malesia and Polynesia (which included the combined islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia in his definition).[^cite_note-89] Publication *Missionary Review of the World* claimed in 1895 that Oceania was divided into five groups: Australasia, Malesia, Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia. It did not consider Hawaii to be part of Polynesia, due to its geographic isolation, commenting that Oceania also included, "isolated groups and islands, such as the Hawaiian and [Galápagos](171118.md)."[^cite_note-missionary-90] In 1876, French geographer [Élisée Reclus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89lis%C3%A9e_Reclus) described Australia's plant life as highly distinctive and noted that Hawaii and the Galápagos had significant numbers of unique, endemic plant species.[^cite_note-91] [Rand McNally & Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rand_McNally), an American publisher of maps and atlases, defined Oceania as including Australia and the Pacific islands while classifying the Malay Archipelago as part of Asia.[^cite_note-92] British linguist [Robert Needham Cust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Needham_Cust) argued in 1887 that the Malay Archipelago should be excluded since it had participated in Asian civilization.[^cite_note-ess-93] Cust considered Oceania's four subregions to be Australasia, Melanesia, Micronesia and Polynesia.[^cite_note-ess-93] New Zealand was categorized by him as being in Polynesia; and the only country in his definition of Australasia was Australia.[^cite_note-ess-93] His definition of Polynesia included both Easter Island and Hawaii, which had not yet been annexed by either Chile or the United States.[^cite_note-ess-93]

The *[Journal of the Royal Statistical Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journal_of_the_Royal_Statistical_Society)* stated in 1892 that Australia was a large island within Oceania rather than a small continent. It additionally commented:

> It is certainly not necessary to consider the [Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_archipelago) and [Australia](4689264.md) as being in the same part of the world; it is, however, permissible to unite in one group all the islands which are scattered over the great ocean. It should be remarked that if we take the Malay Archipelago away from Oceania, as do generally the German geographers, the insular world contained in the great ocean is cut in two, and the least populated of the five parts of the world is diminished to increase the number of inhabitants of the most densely populated continent.[^cite_note-94]

Regarding Australia and the Pacific, *Chambers's New Handy Volume American Encyclopædia* observed in 1885 that, the term "Oceania" has been used interchangeably with "Australasia," though often excluding the Malay Archipelago, which some writers referred to as "[Malesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malesia)".[^cite_note-chambers-95] It added there was controversy over the exact limits of Oceania, saying that, the encyclopaedia mentioned the lack of agreement among geographers about the exact boundaries of Oceania.[^cite_note-chambers-95] British physician and ethnologist [James Cowles Prichard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cowles_Prichard) stated that the Aleutian and [Kuril Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuril_Islands), along with the coasts of Asia and America, marked the northern boundary of Oceania. However, Prichard argued that these islands were generally not considered part of Oceania because their inhabitants were not connected to the indigenous peoples of the more remote Pacific islands. He added that Hawaii was the most northerly area to be inhabited by races associated with Oceania.[^cite_note-96]

The 1926 book *Modern World History, 1776\&ndash- 1926*, by Alexander Clarence Flick, considered Oceania to include all islands in the Pacific, and associated the term with the Malay Archipelago, the islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia, the Aleutian Islands, Japan's [Ryukyu Islands](8458939.md), Taiwan, and the Kuril Islands.[^cite_note-flick-97] He further included in his definition [Sakhalin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sakhalin), an island which is geologically part of the [Japanese Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_archipelago), but that has been administered by Russia since [World War II](32927.md). Hong Kong, partly located in another marginal sea of the Pacific (the [South China Sea](74209.md)), was also included in his definition. Australia and New Zealand were grouped by Flick as Australasia and categorized as being in the same area of the world as the islands of Oceania. Flick described the demographic composition of Oceania, stating that the majority of the population consisted of "brown and yellow races," while whites were primarily "owners and rulers."[^cite_note-flick-97] Hutton Webster's 1919 book *Medieval and Modern History&#x20;*&#x61;lso defined Oceania broadly, stating that it encompassed all islands in the Pacific. Webster broke Oceania up into two subdivisions: the continental group, which included Australia, the Japanese archipelago, the Malay Archipelago, and Taiwan, and the oceanic group, which included New Zealand and the islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia.[^cite_note-98]

Charles Marion Tyler's 1885 book *The Island World of the Pacific Ocean* considered Oceania to ethnographically encompass Australia, New Zealand, the Malay Archipelago, and the islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia. However, Tyler included other Pacific islands in his book as well, such as the Aleutian Islands, the Bonin Islands, the Japanese archipelago, the Juan Fernández Islands, the Kuril Islands, the Ryukyu Islands, Taiwan, [California](5407.md)'s [Channel Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Islands_\(California\)) and [Farallon Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farallon_Islands), [Canada](5042916.md)'s [Vancouver Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vancouver_Island) and [Queen Charlotte Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haida_Gwaii) (now known as Haida Gwaii), Chile's [Chiloé Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chilo%C3%A9_Island), Ecuador's Galápagos Islands, Mexico's [Guadalupe Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guadalupe_Island), [Revillagigedo Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revillagigedo_Islands), [San Benito Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islas_San_Benito) and [Tres Marías Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islas_Mar%C3%ADas), and Peru's [Chincha Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chincha_Islands).[^cite_note-tyler-99] Islands in marginal seas of the Pacific were also covered in the book, including Alaska's [Pribilof Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pribilof_Islands) and China's [Hainan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainan). Tyler additionally profiled the [Anson Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anson_Archipelago), which during the 19th century was a designation for a widely scattered group of purported islands in the Northwestern Pacific Ocean between Japan and Hawaii. The Anson archipelago included [phantom islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phantom_island) such as [Ganges Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges_Island) and [Los Jardines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Jardines) which were proven to not exist, as well as real islands such as Marcus Island and Wake Island.[^cite_note-100][^cite_note-101] Tyler described Australia as "the [leviathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leviathan) of the island groups of the world".[^cite_note-tyler-99] In his 1857 book *A Treatise on Physical Geography*, Francis B. Fogg defined Oceania, or "the Maritime World," as being divided into three major subregions: Australasia, Malesia, and Polynesia. Fogg defined Polynesia as covering the combined islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia, as well as the Ryukyu Islands. He listed additional significant islands in the Pacific beyond those in the standard subregions of Oceania.[^cite_note-102] Scottish academic [John Merry Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Merry_Ross) in 1879 considered Polynesia to cover the entire South and Central Pacific area, not just islands ethnographically within Polynesia. He wrote in *The Globe Encyclopedia of Universal Information* that, in its broadest sense, Polynesia could include islands spanning from Sumatra to the Galápagos, along with Australia.[^cite_note-suma-103] Ross acknowledged that Oceania had historically been applied to this vast region but noted that, by his time, the Malay Archipelago was often excluded.[^cite_note-suma-103]

#### Historical and contemporary interpretations

In a 1972 article for the *[Music Educators Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_Educators_Journal)* titled *Musics of Oceania*, author Raymond F. Kennedy wrote:

> Many meanings have been given to the word Oceania. The most inclusive–but not always the most useful–embraces about 25,000 land areas between Asia and the Americas. A more popular and practical definition excludes Indonesia, East Malaysia (Borneo), the Philippines, Taiwan, Japan, and other islands closely related to the Asian mainland, as well as the Aleutians and the small island groups situated near the Americas. Thus, Oceania most commonly refers to the land areas of the South and Central Pacific.[^cite_note-journal-104]

Kennedy defined Oceania as including Australasia, Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia.[^cite_note-journal-104] The [U.S. Government Publishing Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Government_Publishing_Office)'s *Area Handbook for Oceania* from 1971 states that Australia and New Zealand are the principal large sovereignties of the area. It further states:

> In its broadest definition, Oceania embraces all islands and island groups of the Pacific Ocean that lie between Asia and the two American continents. In popular usage, however, the designation has a more restricted application. The islands of the North Pacific, such as the Aleutians and the Kuriles, are usually excluded. In addition, the series of sovereign island nations fringing Asia (Japan, Taiwan, the Philippines, East Malaysia, the Republic of Indonesia) is not ordinarily considered to be part of the area.[^cite_note-handbook-105]

In 1948, American military journal *Armed Forces Talk* broke the islands of the Pacific up into five major subdivisions: Indonesia, Melanesia, Micronesia, Polynesia, and the non-tropical Islands. The Indonesian subdivision consisted of the islands of the Malay Archipelago, while the non-tropical islands were categorized as being North Pacific islands, such as Alaska's [Kodiak archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kodiak_Archipelago), the Aleutian Islands, Japan, the Kuril Islands, and Sakhalin. Japan's Bonin and Ryukyu Islands are also considered to be [subtropical islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropics), with the main Japanese archipelago being non-tropical.[^cite_note-106] The journal associated the term Oceania with the Melanesian, Micronesian, and Polynesian subdivisions, but not with the Indonesian or non-tropical subdivisions.[^cite_note-107] The *Pacific Islands Handbook* (1945), by Robert William Robson, stated that, "Pacific Islands generally are regarded as Pacific islands lying within the tropics. There are a considerable number of Pacific Islands outside the tropics. Most of them have little economic or political importance." He noted the political significance of the Aleutian Islands, which were invaded by the Japanese military in World War II, and categorized New Zealand's [Antipodes Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antipodes_Islands), [Auckland Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auckland_Islands), [Bounty Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bounty_Islands), [Campbell Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campbell_Islands), [Chatham Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_Island) and [Kermadec Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kermadec_Islands) as being non-tropical islands of the South Pacific, along with Australia's [Lord Howe Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_Howe_Island) and Norfolk Island. The Kermadec Islands, Lord Howe Island, and Norfolk Island are also considered to be subtropical islands. Other non-tropical areas below the equator, such as Chiloé Island, Macquarie Island, Tasmania, and the southern portions of mainland Australia and New Zealand, were not included in this category.[^cite_note-108]

According to the 1998 book *Encyclopedia of Earth and Physical Sciences*, Oceania refers to Australasia, Melanesia, Micronesia, Polynesia, and more than 10,000 islands scattered across the Pacific Ocean. It notes that, "the term \[has] also come under scrutiny by many geographers. Some experts insist that Oceania encompasses even the cold Aleutian Islands and the islands of Japan. Disagreement also exists over whether or not Indonesia, the Philippines, and Taiwan should be included in Oceania."[^cite_note-109] The Japanese Archipelago, the Malay Archipelago and Taiwan and other islands near China are often deemed as a geological extension of Asia, since they do not have [oceanic geology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceanic_islands), instead being detached fragments of the Eurasian continent that were once [physiographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_geography) connected.[^cite_note-110][^cite_note-chen-111][^cite_note-112] Certain Japanese islands off the main archipelago are not geologically associated with Asia.[^cite_note-113][^cite_note-114] The book *The World and Its Peoples: Australia, New Zealand, Oceania* (1966) asserts that, "Japan, Taiwan, the Aleutian Islands, Indonesia, the Philippines and Malaysia \[and] the Pacific archipelagos bordering upon the [Far East Asian](19605700.md) mainland are excluded from Oceania", and that "all the islands lying between Australia and the Americas, including Australia, are part of Oceania."[^cite_note-nz-115] Furthermore, the book adds that Hawaii is still within Oceania, despite being politically integrated into the U.S., and that the Pacific Ocean "gives unity to the whole" since "all these varied lands emerge from or border upon the Pacific."[^cite_note-nz-115]

The 1876 book *The Countries of the World*, by British scientist and explorer [Robert Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Brown_\(botanist,_born_1842\)), labelled the Malay Archipelago as Northwestern Oceania, but Brown still noted that these islands belonged more to the Asian continent. They are now often referred to as [Maritime Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Southeast_Asia), with Indonesia, Malaysia, the Philippines, and Singapore being founding members of the [ASEAN](106539.md) regional organization for [Southeast Asia](28741.md) in 1967 (Brunei and East Timor did not exist as independent nations at that point).[^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71][^cite_note-116] Brown also categorized Japan and Taiwan as being in the same part of the world as the islands of Oceania, and excluded them from *The Countries of the World: Volume 5*, which covered mainland Asia and Hong Kong.[^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71] However, Brown did not explicitly associate Japan or Taiwan with the term Oceania.[^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71] He divided Oceania into two subregions: Eastern Oceania, which included the islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia, and Southwestern Oceania, which included Australia and New Zealand.[^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71] The Galápagos Islands, the Juan Fernández Islands, and the Revillagigedo Islands were identified as the easternmost areas of Oceania in the book. Brown wrote, "they lie nearest the American continent of all oceanic islands, and though rarely associated with Polynesia, and never appearing to have been inhabited by any aboriginal races, are, in many ways, remarkable and interesting."[^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71] Brown went on to add, "the small islands lying off the continent, like the [Queen Charlotte's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Charlotte_Islands) in the North Pacific, the [Farallones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farallon_Islands) off California, and the [Chinchas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chincha_Islands) off Peru are—to all intents and purposes, only detached bits of the adjoining shores. But in the case of the Galápagos, at least, this is different."[^cite_note-countriesoftheworld-71] The Juan Fernández Islands and the neighbouring [Desventuradas Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desventuradas_Islands) are today seen as the easternmost extension of the Indo-West Pacific biogeographic region. The islands lie on the Nazca Plate with Easter Island and the Galápagos Islands, and have a significant south-central Pacific component to their marine fauna.[^cite_note-marine-117][^cite_note-east-118][^cite_note-class-65] According to scientific journal *[PLOS One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PLOS_One)*, the [Humboldt Current](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Humboldt_Current) helps create a biogeographic barrier between the marine fauna of these islands and South America.[^cite_note-marine-117] Chile's government have occasionally considered them to be within Oceania along with Easter Island.[^cite_note-119] Chile's government also categorizes Easter Island, the Desventuradas Islands, and the Juan Fernández Islands as being part of a region titled [Insular Chile](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Insular_Chile). They further include in this region Salas y Gómez, a small uninhabited island to the east of Easter Island. *PLOS One* describes Insular Chile as having "cultural and ecological connections to the broader insular Pacific."[^cite_note-marine-117]



A map of member states for the [Pacific Islands Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Islands_Forum), the member states are depicted in blue. The PIF is a governing organization for the Pacific, and all of its members are seen as being politically within Oceania. Territories ethnographically associated with Oceania, but not politically associated with Oceania, such as Easter Island, Hawaii, and Western New Guinea, have considered gaining representation in the PIF. The Pacific island nations of Indonesia, Japan, Malaysia, the Philippines, Singapore, and Taiwan are dialogue partners, but none have full membership. East Timor, located in the marginal seas of the Pacific and Indian Oceans, also has observer status.



An [exclusive economic zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exclusive_economic_zone) map of the Pacific which includes areas not politically associated with Oceania, that may be considered geographically or geologically within Oceania

In her 1997 book *Australia and Oceania*, Australian historian [Kate Darian-Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kate_Darian-Smith) defined the area as covering Australia, New Zealand, and the islands of Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia. She excluded Hawaii from her definition, but not Easter Island.[^cite_note-120] The [International Union for Conservation of Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Union_for_Conservation_of_Nature) stated in a 1986 report that they include Easter Island in their definition of Oceania "based on its Polynesian and biogeographic affinities even though it is politically apart", further noting that other oceanic islands administered by Latin American countries had been included in definitions of Oceania.[^cite_note-121] In 1987, *The Journal of Australasian Cave Research* described Oceania as being "the region from [Irian Jaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irian_Jaya) (Western New Guinea, a province of New Guinea) in the west to Galápagos Islands (Equador) and Easter Island (Chile) in the east."[^cite_note-122] In a 1980 report on [venereal diseases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexually_transmitted_infection) in the South Pacific, the *British Journal of Venereal Diseases* categorized the Desventuradas Islands, Easter Island, the Galápagos Islands and the Juan Fernández Islands as being in an eastern region of the South Pacific, along with areas such as Pitcairn Islands and French Polynesia, but noted that the Galápagos Islands were not a member of the [South Pacific Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Community), like other islands in the South Pacific.[^cite_note-123] The South Pacific Commission is a developmental organization formed in 1947 and is currently known as the Pacific Community; its members include Australia and other Pacific Islands Forum members. In a 1947 article on the formation of the South Pacific Commission for the *Pacific Affairs* journal, author Roy E. James stated the organization's scope encompassed all non-self-governing islands below the equator to the east of Papua New Guinea (which itself was included in the scope and then known as [Dutch New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_New_Guinea)). Easter Island and the Galápagos Islands were defined by James as falling within the organization's geographical parameters.[^cite_note-124] The 2007 book *Asia in the Pacific Islands: Replacing the West*, by New Zealand Pacific scholar [Ron Crocombe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Crocombe), defined the term "Pacific Islands" as being islands in the South Pacific Commission, and stated that such a definition "does not include Galápagos and other \[oceanic] islands off the Pacific coast of the Americas; these were uninhabited when Europeans arrived, then integrated with a South American country and have almost no contact with other Pacific Islands." He adds, "Easter Island still participates in some Pacific Island affairs because its people are Polynesian."[^cite_note-asianpacific-22]

[Thomas Sebeok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Sebeok)'s two volume 1971 book *Linguistics in Oceania* defines Easter Island, the Galápagos Islands, the Juan Fernández Islands, [Costa Rica](5551.md)'s [Cocos Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocos_Island) and [Colombia](5222.md)'s [Malpelo Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malpelo_Island) (all oceanic)[^cite_note-zug-125] as making up a [Spanish language](26825.md) segment of Oceania.[^cite_note-ling-126] Cocos Island and Malpelo Island are the only landmasses located on the [Cocos Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocos_Plate), which is to the north of the Nazca Plate. The book observed that a native Polynesian language was still understood on Easter Island, unlike on the other islands, which were uninhabited when discovered by Europeans and were mostly being used as prisons for convicts.[^cite_note-ling-126] Additionally, the book includes Taiwan and the entire Malay Archipelago as part of Oceania.[^cite_note-lingtai-127] While not oceanic in nature, Taiwan and Malay Archipelago countries like Indonesia and the Philippines share [Austronesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesians) ethnolinguistic origins with Melanesia, Micronesia and Polynesia, hence their inclusion in the book.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-lingtai-127] Hainan, which neighbours Taiwan, also has Austronesian ethnolinguistic origins, although it was not included in the book.[^cite_note-129] The book defined Oceania's major subregions as being Australia, Indonesia (which included all areas associated with the Malay Archipelago), Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia. In 2010, Australian historian Bronwen Douglas claimed in *The Journal of Pacific History* that "a strong case could be made for extending Oceania to at least Taiwan, the homeland of the Austronesian language family whose speakers colonized significant parts of the region about 6,000 years ago."[^cite_note-130] For political reasons, Taiwan was a member of the [Oceania Football Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oceania_Football_Confederation) during the 1970s and 1980s, rather than the [Asian Football Confederation](715249.md).[^cite_note-131][^cite_note-132]

Ian Todd's 1974 book *Island Realm: A Pacific Panorama* also defines oceanic Latin American islands as making up a Spanish language segment of Oceania, and includes the Desventuradas Islands, Easter Island, the Galápagos Islands, Guadalupe Island, the Juan Fernández Islands, the Revillagigedo Islands, and Salas y Gómez. Cocos Island and Malpelo Island were not explicitly referenced in the book, despite being areas that would fall within this range. All other islands associated with Latin American countries were excluded, as they are continental in nature, unlike Guadalupe Island and the Revillagigedo Islands (both situated on the Pacific Plate) and the oceanic islands situated on the Cocos Plate and Nazca Plate. Todd defined the oceanic Bonin Islands as making up a [Japanese language](15606.md) segment of Oceania, and excluded the main Japanese archipelago.[^cite_note-realm-69] Todd further included the Aleutian Islands in his definition of Oceania. The island chain borders both the Pacific Plate and the [North American Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Plate), and is geologically a partially submerged volcanic extension of the [Aleutian Range](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleutian_Range) on the Alaskan mainland.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-EB1911-135] He did not include the volcanic Kuril Islands and Ryukyu Islands, which similarly border both the Eurasian Plate and the Pacific Plate,[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137] nor did he include the neighbouring Kodiak archipelago in the North Pacific Ocean, which is firmly situated on the North American Plate.[^cite_note-138] *The Stockholm Journal of East Asian Studies* stated in 1996 that Oceania was defined as Australia and an ensemble of various Pacific Islands, "particularly those in the central and south Pacific \[but] never those in the extreme north, for example the Aleutian chain."[^cite_note-139] In the *Pacific Ocean Handbook* (1945), author Eliot Grinnell Mears claimed, "it is customary to exclude the Aleutians of the North Pacific, the American coastal islands and the [Netherlands East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netherlands_East_Indies)", and that he included Australia and New Zealand in Oceania for "scientific reasons; Australia's fauna is largely continental in character, New Zealand's are clearly insular; and neither [Commonwealth realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations) has close ties with Asia."[^cite_note-140] In his 2002 book *Oceania: An Introduction to the Cultures and Identities of Pacific Islanders*, Andrew Strathern excluded [Okinawa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Okinawa_Island) and the rest of the Ryukyu Islands from his definition of Oceania, but noted that the islands and their [indigenous inhabitants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ryukyuan_people) "show many parallels with Pacific island societies."[^cite_note-141]

In the 2006 book *Extinction and Biogeography of Tropical Pacific Birds*, American paleontologist [David Steadman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Steadman) wrote, "no place on earth is as perplexing as the 25,000 islands that make Oceania." Steadman viewed Oceania as encompassing Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia (including Easter Island and Hawaii). He excluded from his definition the larger islands of New Guinea and New Zealand, and argued that Cocos Island, the Galápagos Islands, the Revillagigedo Islands, and other oceanic islands near the Americas were not part of Oceania, due to their biogeographical affinities with that area and lack of prehistoric indigenous populations.[^cite_note-birds-61] In his 2018 book *Regionalism in South Pacific*, Chinese author Yu Changsen wrote that some "stress a narrow vision of the Pacific as those Pacific Islands excluding Australia and even sometimes New Zealand", adding that the term Oceania "promotes a broader concept that has room for Australia and New Zealand."[^cite_note-142]

American marine geologist Anthony A. P. Koppers wrote in the 2009 book *Encyclopedia of Islands* that, "as a whole, the islands of the Pacific Region are referred to as Oceania, the tenth continent on earth. Inherent to their remoteness and because of the wide variety of island types, the Pacific Islands have developed unique social, biological, and geological characteristics." Koppers considered Oceania to encompass the entire 25,000 islands of the Pacific Ocean. In this book, he included the Aleutian Islands, the Galápagos Islands, the Japanese archipelago, the Kuril Islands and continental islands off the coast of the Americas such as the Channel Islands, the Farallon Islands and Vancouver Island;[^cite_note-143] all of these islands lie in or close to the Pacific [Ring of Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ring_of_Fire), as is the case with New Guinea and New Zealand, which were also included. In the 2013 book *The Environments of the Poor in Southeast Asia, East Asia and the Pacific*, Paul Bullen critiqued the definition of Oceania in *Encyclopedia of Islands*, and wrote that since Koppers included areas such as Vancouver Island, it is "not clear what the referents of 'Pacific Region', 'Oceania' or 'Pacific Islands' are." Bullen added, "Asia, Europe, and the Maritime Continent are not literal geographic continents. The '[Asia–Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia%E2%80%93Pacific) region' would comprise two quasi-continents. 'The Pacific' would not refer to the Pacific Ocean and everything in it, e.g., the Philippines."[^cite_note-144] *The Concise Oxford Dictionary of World Place Names* (2017), by [John Everett-Heath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Everett-Heath), states that Oceania is "a collective name for more than 10,000 islands in the Pacific Ocean" and that "it is generally accepted that Indonesia, Japan, the Philippines, Taiwan, and the islands north of Japan (the Kurils and Aleutians) are excluded."[^cite_note-ev-145] In his 1993 book *A New Oceania: Rediscovering Our Sea of Islands*, New Guinea-born [Fijian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fijians) scholar [Epeli Hauʻofa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epeli_Hau%CA%BBofa) wrote that, "Pacific Ocean islands from Japan, through the Philippines and Indonesia, which are adjacent to the Asian mainland, do not have oceanic cultures, and are therefore not part of Oceania."[^cite_note-edu_au-27]

*The Oxford Handbook of Prehistoric Oceania* (2018) defined Oceania as only covering Austronesian-speaking islands in Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia, with this definition including New Guinea and New Zealand. Other Austronesian areas, such as Indonesia and the Philippines, were not included due to their closer cultural proximity to mainland Asia. Australia was also not included, as it was settled several thousand of years before the arrival of Austronesian-speaking peoples in Melanesia, Micronesia, and Polynesia. The book stated, "this definition of Oceania might seem too restrictive: Why not include Australia, for example, or even too broad, for what does [Highland New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea_Highlands) have to do with Hawaiʻi?", further noting that, "a few other islands in the Pacific such as those of Japan or the Channel Islands off the southern California coast are not typically considered Oceania as the indigenous populations of these places do not share a common ancestry with Oceanic groups, except for a time far before humans sailed Pacific waters."[^cite_note-146] It has been theorized that the indigenous [Jōmon people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/J%C5%8Dmon_people) of the Japanese archipelago are related to Austronesians, along with the indigenous inhabitants of the Ryukyu Islands. Some also theorize that [Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australian) are related to the [Ainu people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ainu_people), who are the original inhabitants of Japan's [Hokkaido](58092.md), the Kuril Islands and the southern part of Sakhalin.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148] In their 2019 book *Women and Violence: Global Lives in Focus*, Kathleen Nadeau and Sangita Rayamajhi wrote:

> The Philippines, Malaysia, Brunei, and most of Indonesia are not usually considered to be part of the region of Oceania as it is understood today. These regions are usually considered to be part of Maritime Southeast Asia. These regions, as well as the large East Asian islands of Taiwan, Hainan, and the Japanese archipelago, have varying degrees of cultural connections.[^cite_note-149]

In *Reptiles and Amphibians of the Pacific Islands: A Comprehensive Guide* (2013), [George R. Zug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Robert_Zug) claimed that "a standard definition of Oceania includes Australia, Papua New Guinea, the Solomon Islands, New Caledonia, and New Zealand, and the oceanic islands of Polynesia, Micronesia, and Melanesia." He went on to write that his preferred definition of Oceania emphasizes islands with oceanic geology, stating that oceanic islands are, "islands with no past connections to a continental landmass" and that, "these boundaries encompass the Hawaiian and Bonin Islands in the north and Easter Island in the south, and the [Palau](23372.md) Islands in the west to the Galápagos Islands in the east."[^cite_note-zug-125] Australia, New Guinea, New Zealand and New Caledonia (which is geologically associated with New Zealand) were all excluded, as these areas are descendants of the ancient [Pangaea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pangaea) supercontinent, along with landmasses such as the Americas and [Afro-Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Eurasia). Volcanic islands which are geologically associated with continental landmasses, such as the Aleutian Islands, Japan's [Izu Islands](728303.md), the Kuril Islands, the Ryukyu Islands, and most of the Solomon Islands, were also excluded from his definition. Unlike the United Nations, the World Factbook defines the still-uninhabited Clipperton Island as being a discrete political entity, and they categorize it as part of North America, presumably due to its relative proximity (situated 1,200 kilometres off Mexico on the Pacific Plate). Clipperton is not politically associated with the Americas, as is the case with other oceanic islands near the Americas, having had almost no interaction with the continent throughout its history.[^cite_note-150][^cite_note-151] From the early 20th century to 2007, the island was administratively part of French Polynesia, which itself was known as French Oceania up until 1957.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153] In terms of marine fauna, Clipperton shares similarities with areas of the Pacific which are much farther removed from the Americas.[^cite_note-clipzoo-154][^cite_note-155] Scottish author [Robert Hope Moncrieff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Hope_Moncrieff) considered Clipperton to be the easternmost point of Oceania in 1907, while Ian Todd also included it in his definition of Oceania in *Island Realm: A Pacific Panorama*.[^cite_note-156]

Other uninhabited Pacific Ocean landmasses have been explicitly associated with Oceania,[^cite_note-janick-157] including the highly remote Baker Island and Wake Island (now administered by the [U.S. military](32212.md)).[^cite_note-stats-72] This is due to their location in the centre of the Pacific, their biogeography and their oceanic geology. Less isolated oceanic islands that were once uninhabited, such as the Bonin Islands, the Galápagos Islands and the Juan Fernández Islands, have since been sparsely populated by citizens of their political administrators.[^cite_note-realm-69][^cite_note-ling-126] Archaeological evidence suggests that [Micronesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micronesians) may have lived on the Bonin Islands c. 2,000 years ago, but they were uninhabited at the time of European discovery in the 16th century.[^cite_note-158]

#### Boundaries between subregions

Depending on the definition, New Zealand could be part of Polynesia, or part of Australasia with Australia.[^cite_note-159] New Zealand was originally settled by the Polynesian [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_people), and has long maintained a political influence over the subregion.[^cite_note-southsea-160][^cite_note-lowy18-161] Through immigration and high Māori birth rates, New Zealand has attained the largest population of Polynesians in the world,[^cite_note-162] while Australia has the third largest Polynesian population (consisting entirely of immigrants). Modern-day Indigenous Australians are loosely related to Melanesians,[^cite_note-163][^cite_note-164] and Australia maintains political influence over Melanesia,[^cite_note-lowy18-161] which is mostly located on the same tectonic plate.[^cite_note-birds-61][^cite_note-plates-62] Despite this, Australia is rarely seen as a part of the subregion.[^cite_note-165][^cite_note-Codrington_Melanesians_Encyc-166] As with Australia and New Zealand, Melanesia's New Caledonia has a significant non-indigenous European population, numbering around 71,000.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-167] Conversely, New Caledonia has still had a similar history to the rest of Melanesia, and their French-speaking Europeans make up only 27% of the total population.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-167][^cite_note-devchris-168] As such, it is not also culturally considered a part of the predominantly English-speaking Australasia.[^cite_note-emb-169] Some cultural and political definitions of Australasia include most or all of Melanesia, due to its geographical proximity to Australia and New Zealand, but these are rare.[^cite_note-NZOD-170] Australia, New Zealand and the islands of Melanesia are more commonly grouped together as part of the [Australasian biogeographical realm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australasian_realm).[^cite_note-171] Papua New Guinea is geographically the closest country to Australia, and is often geologically associated with Australia as it was once physiologically connected.[^cite_note-stats-72] Australia's Indian Ocean external territories of [Christmas Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christmas_Island) and [Cocos (Keeling) Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cocos_\(Keeling\)_Islands) are situated within the bounds of the Australian Plate and have been geographically associated with Southeast Asia, due to their proximity to western Indonesia.[^cite_note-172][^cite_note-173][^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175][^cite_note-176] Both were uninhabited when discovered by Europeans during the 17th century. Approximately half of the population on these islands are [European Australian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Australians) mainlanders (with smaller numbers being [European New Zealanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_New_Zealanders)), while the other half are immigrants from China or the nearby Malay Archipelago.[^cite_note-Census2016-177][^cite_note-178] Australia's Indian Ocean external territory Heard Island and McDonald Islands lie on the [Antarctic Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctic_Plate) and are also thought of as being in Antarctica or no region at all, due to their extreme geographical isolation.[^cite_note-geo-179][^cite_note-stats-72] The World Factbook define Heard Island and McDonald Islands as part of Antarctica, while placing Christmas Island and Cocos (Keeling) Islands as the westernmost extent of Oceania.[^cite_note-180][^cite_note-181]

[Norfolk Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norfolk_Island), an external territory of Australia, was inhabited in prehistoric times by either Melanesians or Polynesians, and is geographically adjacent to the islands of Melanesia. The current inhabitants are mostly European Australians, and the UN categorizes it as being in the Australasia subregion.[^cite_note-stats-72] The 1982 edition of the *South Pacific Handbook*, by David Stanley, groups Australia, New Zealand, Norfolk Island, and Hawaiʻi together under an "Anglonesia" category. This is despite the geographical distance separating these areas from Hawaiʻi, which technically lies in the North Pacific.[^cite_note-182] The 1985 edition of the *South Pacific Handbook* also groups the Galápagos Islands as being in Polynesia, while noting that they are not culturally a part of the subregion.[^cite_note-183] The islands are typically grouped with others in the southeastern Pacific that were never inhabited by Polynesians.[^cite_note-184][^cite_note-veg-185]

The Bonin Islands are in the same biogeographical realm as the geographically adjacent Micronesia, and are often grouped in with the subregion because of this.[^cite_note-186][^cite_note-veg-185]

## History

Main articles: [History of Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Oceania) and [History of the Pacific Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Pacific_Islands)

### Colonisation of Oceania

#### Australia

Main articles: [Prehistory of Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory_of_Australia) and [History of Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Indigenous_Australians)



A 19th-century engraving of an Aboriginal Australian encampment

[Indigenous Australians](12598742.md) are the original inhabitants of the Australian continent and nearby islands who migrated from Africa to Asia c. 70,000 years ago[^cite_note-scimag-187] and arrived in Australia c. 50,000 years ago.[^cite_note-illumina.com-188] They are believed to be among the [earliest human migrations out of Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recent_African_origin_of_modern_humans).[^cite_note-BBSRC-189] Although they likely migrated to Australia through Southeast Asia, they are not demonstrably related to any known Asian or Polynesian population.[^cite_note-ourcount-190] There is evidence of genetic and linguistic interchange between Australians in the far north and the Austronesian peoples of modern-day [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea) and the islands, but this may be the result of recent trade and [intermarriage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interracial_marriage).[^cite_note-germs314-191]

They reached [Tasmania](29944.md) c. 40,000 years ago by migrating across a [land bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_bridge) from the mainland that existed during the last [ice age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_age).[^cite_note-prehis-192] It is believed that the first [early human migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_human_migration) to Australia was achieved when this landmass formed part of the [Sahul continent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahul_continent), connected to the island of New Guinea via a land bridge.[^cite_note-perspe81-193] The [Torres Strait Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait_Islanders) are indigenous to the [Torres Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait) Islands, which are at the northernmost tip of [Queensland](59051.md) near [Papua New Guinea](22943.md).[^cite_note-uow2004-194] The earliest definite human remains found in Australia are those of [Mungo Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mungo_Man), which have been dated at c. 40,000 years old.[^cite_note-pmid4570638-195]

It is estimated that 4% to 6% of the genome in Melanesians (e.g., Papua New Guinean and Bougainville Islander) derives from the [Denisova hominin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denisovan), an ancient human species discovered in 2010, while no Eurasians or Africans displayed contributions of the Denisovan genes.[^cite_note-196]

#### Melanesia

Main article: [History of New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Guinea)

The original inhabitants of the group of islands now named Melanesia were likely the ancestors of the present-day [Papuan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papuan_languages)-speaking people. Migrating from Southeast Asia, they appear to have occupied these islands as far east as the main islands in the [Solomon Islands archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_Islands_\(archipelago\)), including [Makira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makira) and possibly the smaller islands farther to the east.[^cite_note-struct2072-197]

Particularly along the north coast of New Guinea and in the islands north and east of New Guinea, the [Austronesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_people), who had migrated into the area somewhat more than 3,000 years ago, came into contact with these pre-existing populations of Papuan-speaking peoples. In the late 20th century, some scholars theorized a long period of interaction, which resulted in many complex changes in genetics, languages, and culture among the peoples.[^cite_note-malenes-198]

#### Micronesia

See also: [History of the Federated States of Micronesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_the_Federated_States_of_Micronesia)



Stone money transport to [Yap Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yap_Island) in Micronesia (1880)



Chronological dispersal of [Austronesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_people) across the Pacific (per Bellwood in Chambers, 2008)

Micronesia began to be settled several millennia ago, although there are competing theories about the origin and arrival of the first settlers. There are numerous difficulties with conducting archaeological excavations in the islands, due to their size, settlement patterns, and storm damage. As a result, much evidence is based on linguistic analysis.[^cite_note-lapita-199]

The earliest archaeological traces of civilization have been found on the island of [Saipan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saipan), dated to 1500 BCE or slightly before. The ancestors of the [Micronesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Micronesians) settled there over 4,000 years ago. A decentralized chieftain-based system eventually evolved into a more centralized economic and religious culture centred on [Yap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yap) and [Pohnpei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pohnpei).[^cite_note-USDOS-200] The prehistories of many Micronesian islands, such as Yap, are not known very well.[^cite_note-books.google-201]

The first people of the Northern Mariana Islands navigated to the islands and discovered it at some period between 4000 BCE to 2000 BCE from [Southeast Asia](28741.md). They became known as the [Chamorros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamorro_people). Their [language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chamorro_language) was named after them. The ancient Chamorro left several megalithic ruins, including [Latte stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latte_stone). The Refaluwasch or [Carolinian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolinian_people) came to the Marianas in the 1800s from the [Caroline Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Islands). Micronesian colonists gradually settled the Marshall Islands during the [2nd millennium BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2nd_millennium_BC)E, with inter-island navigation made possible using [traditional stick charts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Islands_stick_chart).[^cite_note-races165-202]

#### Polynesia

Main articles: [History of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Hawaii) and [History of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Zealand)

Further information: [Polynesian navigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_navigation)



[Moai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moai) at Ahu Tongariki on [Rapa Nui (Easter Island)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island)

The Polynesian people are considered to be, by linguistic, archaeological, and human genetic ancestry, a subset of the sea-migrating [Austronesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_people) and tracing [Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_languages) places their [prehistoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistory) origins in the [Malay Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_Archipelago), and ultimately, in [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Taiwan). Between c. 3000 and 1000 BCE, speakers of [Austronesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages) began spreading from Taiwan into [Island Southeast Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_Southeast_Asia),[^cite_note-matrilineality2003-203][^cite_note-Kayser,_M._2006-204][^cite_note-polyori8225-205] as tribes whose [natives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwanese_aborigines) were thought to have arrived through South China c. 8,000 years ago to the edges of western Micronesia and on into Melanesia.

In the archaeological record, there are well-defined traces of this expansion that allow the path it took to be followed and dated with some certainty. It is thought that by roughly 1400 BCE,[^cite_note-wings-206] "[Lapita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lapita_culture) Peoples", so-named after their pottery tradition, appeared in the [Bismarck Archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bismarck_Archipelago) of north-west Melanesia.[^cite_note-Leach_&_Green_1989-207][^cite_note-burley128-208]

Easter Islanders claimed that a chief [Hotu Matuꞌa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hotu_Matu%EA%9E%8Ca)[^cite_note-vikings228-209] discovered the island in one or two large canoes with his wife and extended family.[^cite_note-hawaii-210] They are believed to have been [Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians). Around 1200, [Tahitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitians) explorers discovered and began settling the area. This date range is based on [glottochronological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glottochronology) calculations and on three [radiocarbon dates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radiocarbon_dating) from charcoal that appears to have been produced during forest clearance activities.[^cite_note-failor89-211] Moreover, a recent study, which included radiocarbon dates from what is thought to be very early material, suggests that the island was discovered and settled as recently as 1200.[^cite_note-scimag23874-212]

### European exploration

See also: [Magellan expedition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magellan_expedition), [Spanish East Indies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_East_Indies), [History of Australia (1788–1850)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Australia_\(1788%E2%80%931850\)), and [Colony of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_New_Zealand)



1852 map of Oceania by J. G. Barbié du Bocage. Includes regions of Polynesia, Micronesia, Melanesia, and [Malesia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malesia).

Oceania was first explored by Europeans from the 16th century onwards. Portuguese navigators, between 1512 and 1526, reached the [Maluku Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maluku_Islands) (by [António de Abreu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ant%C3%B3nio_de_Abreu) and [Francisco Serrão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francisco_Serr%C3%A3o) in 1512), [Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timor), the [Aru Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aru_Islands_Regency) (Martim A. Melo Coutinho), the [Tanimbar Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanimbar_Islands), some of the [Caroline Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caroline_Islands) (by [Gomes de Sequeira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gomes_de_Sequeira) in 1525), and west [Papua New Guinea](22943.md) (by [Jorge de Menezes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_de_Menezes) in 1526). In 1519, a [Spanish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish_Empire) expedition led by [Ferdinand Magellan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ferdinand_Magellan) sailed down the east coast of South America, found and sailed through the [strait that bears his name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strait_of_Magellan), and on 28 November 1520 entered the ocean which he named "Pacific". The three remaining ships, led by Magellan and his captains [Duarte Barbosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duarte_Barbosa) and [João Serrão](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo%C3%A3o_Serr%C3%A3o), then sailed north and caught the [trade winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_winds) which carried them across the Pacific to the Philippines, where Magellan was killed. One surviving ship led by [Juan Sebastián Elcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juan_Sebasti%C3%A1n_Elcano) returned west across the Indian Ocean, and [the other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinidad_\(ship\)) went north in the hope of finding the [westerlies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Westerlies) and reaching Mexico. Unable to find the right winds, it was forced to return to the East Indies. The [Magellan-Elcano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Magellan's_circumnavigation) expedition achieved the first [circumnavigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circumnavigation) of the world and reached the [Philippines](23440.md), the [Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariana_Islands), and other islands of Oceania.

From 1527 to 1595, several other large Spanish expeditions crossed the Pacific Ocean, leading to the arrival in [Marshall Islands](19147.md) and [Palau](23372.md) in the North Pacific, as well as [Tuvalu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tuvalu), the [Marquesas Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquesas_Islands), the [Solomon Islands archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solomon_Islands_\(archipelago\)), the [Cook Islands](7067.md), and the [Admiralty Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admiralty_Islands) in the South Pacific.[^cite_note-Fernandez-Armesto_2006_305–307-213]

In the quest for [Terra Australis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terra_Australis), Spanish explorations in the 17th century, such as the expedition led by the Portuguese navigator [Pedro Fernandes de Queirós](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pedro_Fernandes_de_Queir%C3%B3s), sailed to [Pitcairn](23450.md) and [Vanuatu](32443.md) archipelagos, and sailed the [Torres Strait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Torres_Strait) between Australia and New Guinea, named after navigator [Luís Vaz de Torres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lu%C3%ADs_Vaz_de_Torres). [Willem Janszoon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willem_Janszoon), made the first completely documented European landing in Australia (1606), in [Cape York Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_York_Peninsula).[^cite_note-discos19-214] [Abel Tasman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abel_Tasman) circumnavigated and landed on parts of the Australian continental coast and discovered [Van Diemen's Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Van_Diemen's_Land) (now [Tasmania](29944.md)), New Zealand in 1642, and [Fiji](10707.md).[^cite_note-primary-215] He was the first known European explorer to reach these islands.[^cite_note-tearag-216]

On 23 April 1770, British explorer [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) made his first recorded direct observation of [Aboriginal Australians](2912594.md) at [Brush Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brush_Island) near [Bawley Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bawley_Point,_New_South_Wales).[^cite_note-cook22470-217] On 29 April, Cook and crew made their first landfall on the mainland of the continent at a place now known as the [Kurnell Peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurnell,_New_South_Wales). It is here that James Cook made first contact with an Aboriginal tribe known as the [Gweagal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gweagal). His expedition became the first recorded Europeans to have encountered its eastern coastline of Australia.[^cite_note-herald86292-218]

#### European settlement and colonisation

See also: [History of Australia (1788–1850)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Australia_\(1788%E2%80%931850\)) and [History of New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_New_Zealand)



New Guinea from 1884 to 1919. The Netherlands controlled the western half of New Guinea, [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Empire) the northeastern part, and Britain the southeastern part.

In 1789, the [mutiny on the Bounty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutiny_on_the_Bounty) against [William Bligh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Bligh) led to several of the mutineers escaping the [Royal Navy](26061.md) and settling on [Pitcairn Islands](23450.md), which later became a British colony. Britain also established colonies in Australia in 1788, New Zealand in 1840, and [Fiji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonial_Fiji) in 1872, with much of Oceania becoming part of the [British Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Empire).
